# Abortion law

| --- | --- |
|  | No [gestational limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age) |

|  | Risk to woman's life, to her health\*, rape, or fetal impairment |

&#x20;Note: In some countries or territories, abortion laws are modified by other laws, [regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation), [legal principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_principle) or [judicial decisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_decision). This map shows their combined effect as implemented by the authorities.

**Abortion laws** vary widely among countries and territories, and have changed over time. Such laws range from [abortion](765.md) being freely available on request, to regulation or restrictions of various kinds, to outright prohibition in all circumstances. Many countries and territories that allow abortion have [gestational limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age) for the procedure depending on the reason; with the majority being up to 12 weeks for abortion on request, up to 24 weeks for [rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pregnancy_from_rape), [incest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest), or [socioeconomic reasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomic_status), and more for [fetal impairment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_defect) or risk to the woman's [health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complications_of_pregnancy) or [life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_death). As of 2025, countries that legally allow abortion on request or for socioeconomic reasons comprise about 60% of the world's population. In 2024, [France](5843419.md) became the first country to explicitly protect abortion rights in [its constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_France),[^cite_note-1] while [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia) implicitly inscribed abortion rights in [its constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Yugoslav_Constitution) in 1974.[^cite_note-2]

[Abortion continues to be a controversial subject](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_debate) in many societies on [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_and_abortion), [moral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_aspects_of_the_abortion_debate#Philosophical_argumentation_on_the_moral_issue), ethical, practical, and political grounds. Though it has been banned and otherwise limited by law in many jurisdictions, abortions continue to be common in many areas, even where they are illegal. According to a 2007 study conducted by the [Guttmacher Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guttmacher_Institute) and the [World Health Organization](33583.md), abortion rates are similar in countries where the procedure is legal and in countries where it is not,[^cite_note-kaiser-3][^cite_note-kaiser2-4] due to unavailability of modern [contraceptives](18978770.md) in areas where abortion is illegal.[^cite_note-5] Also according to the study, the number of abortions worldwide is declining due to increased access to contraception.[^cite_note-kaiser-3][^cite_note-kaiser2-4]

## History

Main articles: [History of abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_abortion) and [History of abortion law debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_abortion_law_debate)

Abortion has existed since ancient times, with natural [abortifacients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortifacient) being found amongst a wide variety of tribal people and in most written sources. The earliest known records of abortion techniques and general reproductive regulation date as far back as 2700 BC in [China](5405.md), and 1550 BC in [Egypt](8087628.md).[^cite_note-6] Early texts contain little mention of abortion or abortion law. When it does appear, it is entailed in concerns about male [property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_rights), preservation of social order, and the duty to produce fit citizens for the state or community. The harshest penalties were generally reserved for a woman who procured an abortion against her husband's wishes, and for slaves who produced abortion in a woman of high status. Religious texts often contained severe condemnations of abortion, recommending penance but seldom enforcing secular punishment. As a matter of [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) in [England](9316.md) and the [United States](3434750.md), abortion was illegal anytime after [quickening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quickening)—when the movements of the [fetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fetus) could first be felt by the woman. Under the [born alive rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born_alive_rule), the fetus was not considered a "reasonable being" *in rerum natura*; and abortion was not treated as [murder in English law](3768697.md).

In the 19th century, many Western countries began to codify abortion laws or place further restrictions on the practice. [Anti-abortion movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movement) were led by a combination of groups opposed to abortion on moral grounds, and by medical professionals who were concerned about the danger presented by the procedure and the regular involvement of non-medical personnel in performing abortions. Nevertheless, it became clear that illegal abortions continued to take place in large numbers even where abortions were rigorously restricted. It was difficult to obtain sufficient evidence to prosecute the women and abortion doctors, and judges and juries were often reluctant to convict. For example, [Henry Morgentaler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Morgentaler), a Canadian [pro-choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-choice) advocate, was never convicted by a jury. He was acquitted by a jury in the 1973 court case, but the acquittal was overturned by five judges on the [Quebec Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Court_of_Appeal) in 1974. He went to prison, appealed, and was again acquitted. In total, he served 10 months, suffering a heart attack while in [solitary confinement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solitary_confinement). Many were also outraged at the invasion of privacy and the medical problems resulting from abortions taking place illegally in medically dangerous circumstances. Political movements soon coalesced around the legalization of abortion and [liberalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization) of existing laws.

By the first half of the 20th century, many countries had begun to liberalize abortion laws, at least when performed to protect the woman's life and in some cases on the woman's request. Under [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin), the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) became the first modern state in legalizing abortions on request—the law was first introduced in the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) in 1920, in the [Ukrainian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) in July 1921, and then in the whole country.[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-USSR1995-8] The [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) saw abortion as a social evil created by the capitalist system, which left women without the economic means to raise children, forcing them to perform abortions. The Soviet state initially preserved the [tsarist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) ban on abortion, which treated the practice as [premeditated murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unpremeditated_murder). However, abortion had been practiced by Russian women for decades and its incidence skyrocketed further as a result of the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), which had left the country economically devastated and made it extremely difficult for many people to have children. The Soviet state recognized that banning abortion would not stop the practice because women would continue using the services of private abortionists. In rural areas, these were often old women who had no medical training, which made their services very dangerous to women's health. In November 1920, the Soviet government legalized abortion in state hospitals. The state considered abortion as a temporary necessary evil, which would disappear in the future communist society, which would be able to provide for all the children conceived.[^cite_note-9] In 1936, [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) placed prohibitions on abortions, which restricted them to medically recommended cases only, in order to increase population growth after the enormous loss of life in [World War I](4764461.md) and the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War).[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-USSR1995-8] In the 1930s, several countries ([Poland](22936.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Denmark](76972.md), [Sweden](5058739.md), [Iceland](14531.md), [Mexico](3966054.md)) legalized abortion in some special cases ([pregnancy from rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pregnancy_from_rape), threat to mother's health, fetal malformation). In [Japan](15573.md), abortion was legalized in 1948 by the Eugenic Protection Law,[^cite_note-12] amended in May 1949 to allow abortions for economic reasons.[^cite_note-13] Abortion was legalized in 1952 in [Yugoslavia](34244.md) (on a limited basis), and again in 1955 in the Soviet Union on request. Some [Soviet allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_the_Soviet_Union) (Poland, [Hungary](13275.md), [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czechoslovakia), [Romania](25445.md)) legalized abortion in the late 1950s under pressure from the Soviets.[^cite_note-14]

In the [United Kingdom](31717.md), the [Abortion Act of 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_Act_1967) clarified and prescribed abortions as legal up to 28 weeks (later reduced to 24 weeks). Other countries soon followed, including [Canada](5042916.md) (1969), the United States (1973 in most states, pursuant to *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*—the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) decision which legalized abortion nationwide [until 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)), [Tunisia](30188.md) and Denmark (1973), [Austria](26964606.md) (1974), [France](5843419.md) and Sweden (1975), [New Zealand](4913064.md) (1977), [Italy](14532.md) (1978), the [Netherlands](21148.md) (1984), and [Belgium](3343.md) (1990). However, these countries vary greatly in the circumstances under which abortion was to be permitted.

In 1975, the West German Supreme Court [struck down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Federal_Constitutional_Court_abortion_decision) a law legalizing abortion, holding that it contradicted the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_for_the_Federal_Republic_of_Germany)'s [human rights](13831.md) guarantees. In 1976, a law was adopted which enabled abortions up to 12 weeks. After [Germany's reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_reunification), despite the legal status of abortion in [former East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany), a compromise was reached which deemed most abortions up to 12 weeks legal, but this law was struck down by the [Federal Constitutional Court](34781059.md) and amended only to remove the punishment in such cases, without any statement to legality.

In jurisdictions governed under [sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law), abortion after the 120th day from conception (19 weeks from [LMP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age_\(obstetrics\))) is illegal, especially for those who follow the recommendations of the [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) legal school, while most jurists of the [Maliki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliki) legal school "believe that [ensoulment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensoulment) occurs at the moment of [conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilisation), and they tend to forbid abortion at any point \[similar to the Roman Catholic Church]. The other schools hold intermediate positions. ... The penalty prescribed for an illegal abortion varies according to particular circumstances involved. According to sharia, it should be limited to a fine that is paid to the father or heirs of the fetus."[^cite_note-15]

## Timeline of abortion on request

See also: [Timeline of reproductive rights legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_reproductive_rights_legislation)

The table below lists in chronological order the [United Nations member states](31969.md) that have legalized abortion on request in at least some initial part of the pregnancy, or that have fully [decriminalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decriminalized) abortion. As of 2025, 67 countries have legalized or decriminalized abortion on request.

Where a country has legalized abortion on request, prohibited it, and legalized it again (e.g., former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), [Romania](25445.md)), only the later year is included. Countries that result from the merger of states where abortion on request was legal at the moment of unification show the year when it became legal across the whole [national territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_territory) (e.g., [Germany](11867.md), [Vietnam](202354.md)). Similarly, countries where not all subnational [jurisdictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction) have legalized abortion on request are not included, leading to the exclusion of [Australia](4689264.md), [Mexico](3966054.md), and the [United Kingdom](31717.md). Countries where abortion on request was once legalized nationwide but has since been prohibited in at least part of the country, such as the [United States](3434750.md) and [Poland](22936.md), are also excluded. Countries are counted even if they were not yet independent at the time. The year refers to when the relevant law or [judicial decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_decision) [came into force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Came_into_force), which may be different from the year when it was approved.

## International law

There are no international or multinational treaties that deal directly with abortion but [human rights law](219877.md) and [international criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_criminal_law) touch on the issues.

The [Nuremberg Military Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_Military_Tribunal) decided the case of *[United States v Greifelt and Others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RuSHA_trial)* (1948) on the basis that abortion was a crime within its jurisdiction according to the law defining [crimes against humanity](61099.md) and thus within its definition of murder and extermination.[^cite_note-55]

The [Catholic Church](606848.md) remains highly influential in [Latin America](18524.md), and opposes the legalisation of abortion.[^cite_note-bbc-56] The [American Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Convention_on_Human_Rights), which in 2013 had 23 Latin American parties, declares human life as commencing with conception. In Latin America, abortion on request is only legal in [Cuba](5042481.md) (1965), [Uruguay](31843.md) (2012),[^cite_note-57] [Argentina](18951905.md) (2021),[^cite_note-ar-48] [Colombia](5222.md) (2022)[^cite_note-co2-58] and in parts of [Mexico](3966054.md).[^cite_note-mx1-59][^cite_note-mx2-60] Abortions are completely banned in the [Dominican Republic](8060.md), [El Salvador](9356.md), [Honduras](13394.md) and [Nicaragua](21362.md), and only allowed in certain restricted circumstances in most other Latin American nations.[^cite_note-bbc-56]

In the 2010 case of *[A, B and C v Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A,_B_and_C_v_Ireland)*, the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) found that the [European Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights) did not include a right to an abortion.

In 2005, the [United Nations Human Rights Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee) (UN HRC) ordered [Peru](170691.md) to compensate a woman (known as K.L.) for denying her a [medically indicated abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Termination_for_medical_reasons); this was the first time a United Nations Committee had held any country accountable for not ensuring access to safe, legal abortion, and the first time the committee affirmed that abortion is a human right.[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-61] K.L. received the compensation in 2016.[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-61] In the 2016 case of *[Mellet v Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mellet_v_Ireland)*, the UN HRC found [Ireland's abortion laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland) violated the [International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Civil_and_Political_Rights) because Irish law banned abortion in cases of fatal fetal abnormalities.

## National laws

While abortions are legal at least under certain conditions in almost all countries, these conditions vary widely. According to a [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) report with data gathered up to 2019,[^cite_note-UN2019report-62] abortion is allowed in 98% of countries in order to save a woman's life. Other commonly accepted reasons are preserving physical (72%) or mental health (69%), in cases of rape or incest (61%), and in cases of fetal impairment (61%). Performing an abortion because of economic or social reasons is accepted in 37% of countries. Performing abortion only on the basis of a woman's request is allowed in 34% of countries, including in Canada, most European countries and China.[^cite_note-UN2019report-62]

The exact scope of each legal ground also varies. For example, the laws of some countries cite health risks and fetal impairment as general grounds for abortion and allow a broad interpretation of such terms in practice, while other countries restrict them to a specific list of medical conditions or subcategories. Many countries that allow abortion have [gestational limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age_\(obstetrics\)) for the procedure depending on the reason; with the majority being up to 12 weeks for abortion on request, up to 24 weeks for social, economic, rape, or incest reasons, and more for fetal impairment or threats to the woman's health or life.[^cite_note-UN2019report-62]<sup>: 26 </sup>

In some countries, additional procedures must be followed before the abortion can be carried out even if the basic grounds for it are met. How strictly all of the procedures dictated in the legislation are followed in practice is another matter. For example, in the United Kingdom, a [Care Quality Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Care_Quality_Commission)'s report in 2012 found that several [NHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service) clinics were circumventing the law, using forms pre-signed by one doctor, thus allowing abortions to patients who only met with one doctor.[^cite_note-CQC2012-63]

| Prohibited, with complex legality or [practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unenforced_law) |
| Prohibited |
| Unknown or unclear |

### Comparative limits for countries with elective abortions

| Globe icon. | The examples and perspectives in this section **deal primarily with Europe and North America and do not represent a [worldwide view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:WikiProject_Countering_systemic_bias) of the subject**. You may [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Abortion_law), discuss the issue on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Abortion_law), or create a new section, as appropriate. *(July 2022)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |

Legal limits may not be directly comparable. Limits may be expressed in trimesters, months, weeks of pregnancy ([implantation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Implantation_\(embryology\))), weeks from [fertilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilization), or weeks from [last menstrual period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_menstrual_period) (LMP).

### Countries with more restrictive laws



Supporter of legalized abortion at a rally in [Paraná, Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paran%C3%A1,_Entre_R%C3%ADos). Argentina had restrictive laws until 2021.

According to a report by [Women on Waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_on_Waves), approximately 25% of the world's population lives in countries with "highly restrictive abortion laws"—that is, laws which either completely ban abortion, or allow it only to save the [mother's life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_death). This category includes several countries in [Latin America](18524.md), [Africa](5334607.md), [Asia](689.md) and [Oceania](22621.md), as well as [Andorra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andorra) and [Malta](19137.md) in [Europe](9239.md).[^cite_note-749] The [Center for Reproductive Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Reproductive_Rights) report that "\[t]he inability to access safe and legal abortion care impacts 700 million women of reproductive age."[^cite_note-750]

Some of the countries of [Central America](6121.md), notably [El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_El_Salvador), have also come to international attention due to very forceful enforcement of the laws, including the incarceration of a [gang-rape](1371871.md) victim for homicide when she gave birth to a stillborn son and was accused of attempting an illegal abortion.[^cite_note-751][^cite_note-752][^cite_note-753]

El Salvador has some of the strictest abortion laws of any country. Abortion under all circumstances, including rape, incest, and risk to the mother's health, is illegal. Women can be criminalized and penalized to up to 40 years in prison after being found guilty of an abortion. El Salvador's abortion laws are so severe that miscarriages and stillbirths can sometimes be enough for conviction. The [Inter-American Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Court) has already ruled that El Salvador was responsible for the death of Manuela, who was sentenced to 30 years in prison in 2008 for aggravated homicide after suffering an obstetric emergency that resulted in her losing her pregnancy.[^cite_note-754]

### Beginning of pregnancy controversy

Main article: [Beginning of pregnancy controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beginning_of_pregnancy_controversy)

Controversy over the beginning of pregnancy occurs in different contexts, particularly in a legal context, and is particularly discussed within the [abortion debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_debate) from the point of measuring the [gestational age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age_\(obstetrics\)) of the pregnancy. Pregnancy can be measured from a number of convenient points, including the day of last [menstruation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstrual_cycle), [ovulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulation), [fertilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilisation), [implantation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Implantation_\(human_embryo\)) and chemical detection. A common medical way to calculate gestational age is to measure pregnancy from the first day of the last menstrual cycle.[^cite_note-760] However, not all legal systems use this measure for the purpose of abortion law; for example countries such as [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Belgium), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_France), and [Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Luxembourg) use the term "pregnancy" in the abortion law to refer to the time elapsed from the sexual act that led to [conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conception_\(biology\)), which is presumed to be 2 weeks after the end of the last menstrual period.[^cite_note-761]

## Exceptions in abortion law

Exceptions in abortion laws occur either in countries where abortion is as a general rule illegal or in countries that have abortion on request with gestational limits. For example, if a country allows abortion on request until 12 weeks, it may create exceptions to this general gestation limit for later abortions in specific circumstances.[^cite_note-762]

There are a few exceptions commonly found in abortion laws. Legal domains which do not have abortion on demand will often allow it when the health of the mother is at stake. "Health of the mother" may mean something different in different areas: for example, prior to December 2018, [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland) allowed abortion only to save the mother's life, whereas [abortion opponents in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_anti-abortion_movement) argue health exceptions are used so broadly as to render a ban essentially meaningless.[^cite_note-763]

Laws allowing abortion in cases of rape or [incest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest) often differ. For example, before *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*, thirteen [U.S. states](18618239.md) allowed abortion in the case of either rape or incest, but only [Mississippi](16949861.md) permitted abortion of pregnancies due to rape, and no state permitted it for just incest.[^cite_note-764]

Many countries allow abortion only through the first or second [trimester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trimester_\(pregnancy\)), and some may allow abortion in cases of fetal defects, e.g., [Down syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_syndrome), or where the pregnancy is the result of a [sexual crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_and_the_law).

## Other related laws

Main article: [Legal protection of access to abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_protection_of_access_to_abortion)

Laws in some countries with liberal abortion laws protect access to abortion services. Such legislation often seeks to guard [abortion clinics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_clinic) against [obstruction](293054.md), [vandalism](32676.md), [picketing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picketing_\(protest\)), and other actions, or to protect patients and employees of such facilities from threats and harassment. Other laws create a perimeter around a facility, known variously as a "buffer zone", "bubble zone", or "access zone", where [demonstrations opposing abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movements) are not permitted. Protests and other displays are restricted to a certain distance from the building, which varies depending on the law. Similar zones have also been created to protect the homes of abortion providers and clinic staff. Bubble zone laws are divided into "fixed" and "floating" categories. Fixed bubble zone laws apply to the static area around the facility itself, and floating laws to objects in transit, such as people or [cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car).[^cite_note-cfr-765] Because of conflicts between [anti-abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion) activists on one side and women seeking abortion and medical staff who provides abortion on the other side, some laws are quite strict: in [South Africa](17416221.md) for instance, any person who prevents the lawful termination of a pregnancy or obstructs access to a facility for the termination of a pregnancy faces up to 10 years in prison (section 10.1 (c) of the Choice on Termination of Pregnancy Act[^cite_note-766]).

On 3 November 2020, an association of 20 Kenyan charities urged the government of [Kenya](188171.md) to withdraw from the [Geneva Consensus Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Consensus_Declaration) (GCD), a US-led international accord that sought to limit access to abortion for girls and women around the world. GCD was signed by 33 nations, on 22 October 2020.[^cite_note-767]

| [Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Nepal) | *Lakshmi v. Government of Nepal* [Supreme Court](2707227.md) upheld and expanded legal abortion.[^cite_note-776] |  |  |
| 2011 | [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_Kingdom) | *British Pregnancy Advisory Service v Secretary of State for Health*[^cite_note-777] |  |
| 2012 | [Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Argentina) | *F., A. L.* Abortion allowed in case of rape of any woman, regardless of her mental health.[^cite_note-778] | Yes |
| [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Brazil) | *[ADPF 54](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADPF_54)* Abortion allowed in case of [anencephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anencephaly).[^cite_note-779] |  |  |
| [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) | *[P. and S. v. Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._and_S._v._Poland)*[^cite_note-780] |  |  |

| 2015 | [Dominican Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_Dominican_Republic) | Constitutional Court ruled law allowing abortion in certain cases unconstitutional.[^cite_note-783] | Law restricted |
| [Rwanda](25645.md) | RPA 0787/15/HC/KIG[^cite_note-784] |  |  |
| 2016 | [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States) | *[Whole Woman's Health v. Hellerstedt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_Woman's_Health_v._Hellerstedt)* | Yes |
| [United Nations](31769.md) | *[Mellet v Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mellet_v_Ireland)* |  |  |

| [Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Croatia) | [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Croatia) ruled law allowing abortion on demand constitutional.[^cite_note-785] | Legalization upheld |  |

The decision was implemented on 27 January 2021.[^cite_note-pl2-444] | Law restricted |

| 2021 | [Ecuador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Ecuador) | Abortion allowed in case of rape of any woman, regardless of her mental health.[^cite_note-ec-203] | Yes |
| [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Mexico) | Gestational limit for abortion in case of rape ruled unconstitutional. Ruling only granted relief to plaintiff, did not automatically invalidate law.[^cite_note-mx4-341] |  |  |

Wade* and *Planned Parenthood v. Casey* overturned. | Law restricted |  |
